The seasonal Gaussian process component of the forecasting model proved most beneficial for forecasting performance in epidemiological contexts where changes in transmission were highly consistent year-on-year. We found the inclusion of this component had the greatest performance benefit for RSV, followed by influenza, with only marginal benefit found for SARS-CoV-2. The patterns of RSV transmission are highly seasonal, with the timing of epidemic growth, peak and decline relatively consistent from one year to the next. These stable dynamics were very well captured by our periodic Gaussian process, enabling accurate prediction of RSV case incidence.

While influenza epidemics typically recur at similar times year-to-year, the emergence of new viral strains can produce dynamics that are not well captured by the periodic Gaussian process. This was evident in our application to the 2025 influenza season in Australia, where the unexpected emergence of the influenza A(H3N2) subclade K (Dapat et al. 2025) produced a prolonged period of heightened influenza case incidence. As our model does not capture strain or sub-type-level variation in growth (Davies et al. 2021; Eales et al. 2025), the model instead incorrectly predicted that case incidence would decline following the initial peak. However, the emergence of subclade K was an unusual epidemiological event, and it is possible that the performance of our model in forecasting influenza would be greater in a more typical season. This non-periodicity was most evident for SARS-CoV-2, where epidemics have not followed a stable seasonal pattern across the 2023-2025 years. While these nonperiodic dynamics limited the model's ability to predict changes in growth rate ahead of time, our forecasts still proved capable of adapting to these changes once they were evident in the data. Further, the inclusion of the periodic term is not necessarily harmful for overall performance in such non-periodic settings, as the magnitude of the term can automatically be shrunk towards zero during inference if periodicity is able to be ruled out.

Our estimates of the seasonal Gaussian process f seasonal should not be interpreted as equivalent to a seasonal forcing term on transmission. Instead, this term likely captures a combined effect of changes in both the rate at which the virus can be transmitted (i.e. seasonal forcing, due to changes in human behaviour or viral trans-10 . missibility) and changes in population immunity (with reductions in transmission due to susceptible depletion and increases due to waning immunity). To separate out these components, our model would need to be able to explicitly capture the effects of accumulating immunity (due to exposure) and depleting immunity (due to waning) on the population-scale transmission of respiratory viruses. This remains a difficult task due to the complex dynamics of respiratory virus immunity and our poor understanding of the proportion of infections that are detected in case data.

Accurate forecasts of seasonal respiratory virus burden can aid public health decision-makers in critical areas such as medical workforce scheduling, testing and supply chain planning and public communications (Muscatello et al. 2017; Moss et al. 2018; Frounfelker et al. 2025; Christen et al. 2026). A large number of respiratory virus forecasting programs are currently running (e.g. Gozzi et al. 2026; Hines et al. 2026; Henderson et al. 2026), and improving the performance of these forecasts is an active field of research. Our results show that capturing the effects of school holidays and seasonal changes in transmission can substantially improve the performance of respiratory virus forecasts. Such improvements may be of the greatest utility around peaks in epidemic activity, which in many settings (including ours), often coincide with the onset of school holidays (Cauchemez et al. 2008; Garza et al. 2013). Our model is currently being used to produce real-time forecasts of RSV, influenza and SARS-CoV-2 across eight states and territories in Australia and in New Zealand on a weekly basis as part of the Australia-Aotearoa Winter Situational Assessment Program, with evaluation to follow. Our results are likely to generalise to respiratory virus forecasting work in other settings, and the inclusion of holiday and seasonal effects should be considered where similar patterns in transmission and reporting can be identified.

Methods

Bayesian generative model

We consider the log-transformed effective reproduction number log R(t ) to be made up of five additive components:

The functions ω S (t ) and ω P (t ) capture changes in transmission due to the school holidays and public holidays respectively (e.g. due to changes in contact behaviour), while f seasonal (t ) captures annually-periodic changes in the transmission rate and f noise (t ) captures any variation not explained by the other three terms. The baseline c is included to capture any net difference from log R(t ) = 0 across the fitting period.

For the school holiday term ω S (t ), we estimate two separate effects. The value ω between-term captures any changes to transmission across the shorter between-term school holidays, which are two weeks long and occur every (Southern Hemisphere) autumn, winter and spring, while ω summer captures transmission changes across the summer holidays, which are approximately six weeks long (and coincide with the Christmas and New Year holiday period in Australia). These effects apply whenever time t is in the respective set of time-steps H between-term or H summer . Otherwise, ω S (t ) is zero:

Priors for these parameters (and others) are listed in Table 1.

We estimate a separate effect of each named public holiday in a similar manner. In the case where a public holiday fell on a weekend and was officially observed on the preceding Friday or following Monday, we assumed this could have a different effect on transmission and as such estimated a separate effect here (for example, we estimate one effect for 'New Year's Day' and one for 'New Year's Day (Observed)'). Lists of official public holiday and school holiday dates were compiled from online sources with archived copies of reference material made available in the supplementary data. Differences in holiday dates between jurisdictions for 2025 are presented in Supplementary Figure 1. For simplicity of interpretation, the four public holidays defining the Easter period are treated as a single holiday.

The term f seasonal (t ) captures any seasonal variation in R(t ) that recurs on a periodic, annual basis. We capture this behaviour by modelling f seasonal (t ) as a Gaussian process with a periodic squared-exponential kernel: with ω0 = 2π/365.25 such that the period of the process is 365.25 days.

Finally, the term f noise (t ) captures any variation not more readily explained by the previous three terms. We model this term as a Gaussian process with a Matérn 5/2 kernel: with this kernel selected to capture an intermediate level of smoothness in the noise process.

The latent infection incidence z(t ) is defined as a renewal process (as in Cori et al. 2013; Abbott et al. 2020) across the effective reproduction number R(t ): where g (x) is the probability of an onwards infection occurring after x days, i.e. the generation interval.

Discretised generation interval distributions were taken to be fixed and specified from prior studies for each of the three pathogens modelled (Cauchemez et al. 2009; Vink et al. 2014; Abbott et al. 2022). The value of M was taken to be fixed at 21 days.

To calculate the latent infection incidence as defined above, we must also define z(t ) for the M days prior to the first day of data. We do this by assuming that exponential growth/decay in z(t ) occurs across this period: z(t ) = z(0)e r 0 t , where t < 1, with the initial infection incidence z(0) and growth rate r 0 treated as free model parameters (Table 1).

We assume that the mean case incidence y(t ) is a delayed convolution of the latent infection incidence z(t ). The convolution p delay captures the delay from the onset of infection to case notification date. This distribution is captured as a Log-normal distribution X with a mean of d and a standard-deviation of σ delay . This continuous distribution is truncated at M days and discretised across each day 0 through M -1:

The mean and standard deviation of the untruncated distribution are treated as parameters to be inferred during the fitting process. As the exact timing of infection incidence and thus this distribution is difficult to estimate from case incidence data alone (with only limited information provided by e.g. the timing of public holidays and any corresponding impact on transmission), constrained priors were applied to prevent unrealistically short (or long) delay distributions being inferred (Table 1).

Estimates for p delay from the final round of forecasts (round 2025-11-27) are presented in Supplementary Figure 11.

In addition, we assume that mean case incidence may be increased or reduced during the school holidays via µ S (t ), public holidays via µ P (t ), and according to day-of-week via µ wk (t ):

The school and public holiday reporting effects are estimated in the same manner as that taken for their approach on transmission, with separate effects estimated for the summer and between-term holidays and one effect for each named public holiday. For the day-of-week effect µ wk (t ), we estimate seven values, one for each day of the week: From the mean case incidence y(t ), we assume observed case incidence Y (t ) is drawn from a negative binomial distribution: with dispersion (size) parameter φ.

The full list of model priors is provided in Table 1. For the Gaussian process length-scale priors, we use the approach suggested in (Betancourt 2017), where an inverse Gamma distribution prior is used with parameters selected such that the 99% interval of the distribution covers a desired range. For the seasonal periodic Gaussian process f seasonal , the length-scale ℓ seasonal is specified such that the 99% interval covers the range [14, 90] (i.e. P (14 < ℓ seasonal < 90) ≈ 0.99). The probability density of these inverse Gamma priors drops rapidly outside of these regions, in effect preventing the inference of particularly low length-scales (i.e. over-fitting) without imposing a hard cut-off. Similarly, for f noise , the length-scale ℓ noise is specified such that the 99% interval covers the range [7, 90] (with a lower cut-off chosen here for ℓ noise under the assumption that the noise process may be more flexible than seasonal changes in transmission). model behaviour was similar to that observed in the Australian Capital Territory and Victoria. As such, we excluded these jurisdictions from the analysis for simplicity of presentation.

Inference

Model inference was performed in Stan using the No-U-Turn-Sampler (NUTS) with default parameters (Stan Development Team 2026). For the final model fit (where effect estimates were presented, such as in Figure 5), 5, 000 warm-up iterations followed by 20, 000 sampling iterations were performed across four chains to ensure convergence (with R < 1.01 and effective sample sizes over 400 across all parameters).

To avoid the computational burden of prolonged sampling, prior rounds were initially fit with 2, 000 warm-up and 2, 000 sampling iterations. If R was observed to be greater than 1.05 for these fits, fitting was performed with 5, 000 warm-up iterations and the total number of sampling iterations was increased in increments of 20, 000 until R < 1.05 was achieved (although further sampling produced no discernable difference in forecasting performance). Difficult parameter identification was primarily induced by multimodality in the delay distribution parameters and the public holiday effects. The cmdstanr interface was used to perform all sampling (Gabry et al. 2025).

To improve the computational efficiency of model fitting given the long timeseries used in our model (multiple years of data), the Matérn and periodic Gaussian processes were approximated via Hilbert space Gaussian processes (HSGP) and linear low-rank approximations respectively following the approach presented in Riutort-Mayol et al. (2023). The two methods approximate a Gaussian process with a linear combination of K basis functions, reducing the computational complexity of inference with Gaussian process priors from O (n 3 ) to O (nK + K ), where n is the number of time-points evaluated and K is typically less than n.

Hyper-parameters for the Gaussian process approximations were specified following the guidance provided in Riutort-Mayol et al. (2023). The number of basis functions K for the seasonal Gaussian process f seasonal (t ) was taken to be fixed at 40. For the noise Gaussian process f noise (t ), we calculated the number of basis functions for each model fit using Equation 21 from Riutort-Mayol et al. (2023), with K = 2.65(C * S)/7, where S is half the number of days in the simulation and we assumed a lower bound on the length-scale of 7 days. Finally, the boundary factor C was taken to be 1.25. These hyperparameters were validated to ensure that a highly accurate approximation was produced across the range of length-scales specified by the two priors (Supplementary Figure 12). Stan implementations defining the HSGP basis functions and spectral densities were taken from the EpiNow2 software package (Abbott et al. 2026).

Forecast evaluation

Forecasting performance was evaluated using the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS) (Gneiting et al. 2007) across the sampled case incidence trajectories produced by each model fit. These scores were calculated across log-transformed case incidence counts (specifically y ⋆ = log e (y + 1)) using the scoringRules R package (Jordan et al. 2019). The log transformation makes score more comparable between different settings and means that forecasts that better predict exponential growth rate (or R(t )) are rewarded (Bosse et al. 2023).

To calculate skill scores, we produced a baseline model where case incidence follows a random-walk process, with 7-day seasonality imposed to capture any day-of-week effects (i.e. an ARIMA(0,0,0)(0,1,0) 7 model). Naive forecasts were produced on the natural scale with a lower truncation applied at zero to prevent negative counts. The magnitude of the random walk was informed by case incidence observations for each location-pathogen pair since January 1 2024, and constraints were applied so that projected incidence was non-negative. Overall skill scores were then calculated as one minus the average CRPS for our forecasts divided by the average CRPS of the baseline forecasts across a given range X (defining our aggregation of forecast round, jurisdiction, pathogen, horizon):

For the sensitivity analyses, forecasts without certain components (e.g. the seasonal Gaussian process) were produced by constraining the value of those components to be zero.

Analysis was performed in R (v4.5.0, R Core 2013) using the tidyverse, patchwork, tidybayes, ggtext, arrow and fabletools packages (Wickham et al. 2019; Pedersen 2025; Kay 2024; Wilke and Wiernik 2022; Richardson et al. 2026; O'Hara-Wild et al. 2026).

Data

Forecasts were produced retrospectively across 33 rounds of historical data from the Australia-Aotearoa Forecasting Hub. These rounds spanned the 2025 winter epidemic season in Australia, with each round consisting of a dataset of case incidence data for RSV, influenza virus and SARS-CoV-2. Use of case data for this study was approved by the Health Sciences Human Ethics Sub-Committee, Office for Research

Data and Code Availability

The code used to produce forecasts and figures for this work is available on GitHub at https://github.com/ruarai/reff_comp_public and has been archived at https://doi.org/10.26188/34999054. Anonymised summary data is included to allow for replication of the figures presented. The underlying case datasets used in this work are not publicly available; for access to the raw data, a request must be submitted to the relevant data custodians, which will be assessed independent of the authorship group.

Ethics and Integrity, The University of Melbourne (Application ID 2024-26949-50575-3, "Real-time epidemic analysis and forecasting for decision support").

Linelisted case data was extracted from the Public Health Event Surveillance System (PHESS) for Victoria and from the National Notifiable Disease Surveillance System (NNDSS) for all other jurisdictions. For Victoria, non-electronic case records were excluded to reduce the impact of reporting delays. From these linelists, aggregate case counts were produced for each forecasting round, with truncation performed as necessary to reduce the potential effect of reporting delays. To reduce the impacts of pandemic-related changes in epidemiological dynamics and reporting on our estimates, daily case incidence before 2023-01-01 was removed for RSV and influenza, and before 2023-03-01 for SARS-CoV-2. This later date for SARS-CoV-2 was chosen due to substantial changes in the availability of PCR testing around the start of 2023 (Department of Health, Australia 2022). This daily case incidence is illustrated in Supplementary Figures 2 3 4.

In addition to the use of historically produced data for the rounds spanning from May 2025 through to December 2025, additional synthetic rounds were produced by truncating case incidence data. A total of 12 rounds of RSV data were produced for Queensland and New South Wales starting from February 2025 to capture the early increases in case incidence observed in these jurisdictions (see Supplementary Figure 2) and four rounds of RSV, influenza and SARS-CoV-2 data for Victoria in April and May 2025 to capture the period of early epidemic growth and align coverage with other jurisdictions. We produced results across the jurisdictions of the Australian Capital Territory, New South Wales, the Northern Territory, Queensland and Victoria. While case data was available for South Australia, Tasmania and Western Australia,

Figure 2 :

Figure 2: Performance of our forecasting methodology as retrospectively evaluated over the 2025 winter epidemic season in Australia. A. The performance of our forecast by horizon week, measured using the continuous ranked probability score (CRPS). A lower value indicates better forecasting performance. B. The performance of our forecast at the 4th week horizon (i.e. 22-28 days ahead). STL (Seasonal and Trend decomposition using LOESS) smoothed case incidence is plotted over the CRPS (with scores aligned to the corresponding week of data) to allow for comparison of performance to the underlying epidemiological activity.Where multiple forecasts were produced for the same date (e.g. due to a delay in the receipt of data), the CRPS of the earlier (longer-horizon) forecast is plotted on top. The two labelled forecasts (i, ii) are illustrated in Figure3. C. The relative performance of our forecast as compared to the baseline random-walk model. Skill scores were calculated over the full 28-day-ahead horizon using CRPS over log-counts. A skill score above zero indicates that our forecasts outperform the baseline model, with higher values indicating a greater degree of relative performance.

Percentage changes due to the seasonal and noise Gaussian process effects were calculated as the exponent of the estimated effect minus one.

A negative skill score indicates that removing that component reduces forecasting performance, whereas a positive skill score indicates that removing the component improves performance.
